      10 def guard_generation(run_id, generation):

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● All 10 pass — including the critical rowcount-abort rollback proof.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - test_guard_rolls_back_child_inserts is the linchpin test: a zero-row UPDATE
  is a successful SQL no-op, so without the explicit rowcount == 0 → raise, the
  Category insert in the same transaction would silently commit against a
  superseded run. The raise forces the whole transaction.atomic() block to roll
  back.
  - bump_generation uses SQLite 3.35+ RETURNING to make increment-and-read a
  single atomic statement — no read-modify-write race between overlapping
  refresh and reaper bumps.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Let me update the progress tracker and commit.

● ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - The fan-in's dedup plan is computed entirely in memory before the REPORT
  network call, then applied inside one short fenced transaction — honoring the
  hard rule "never hold a SQLite writer lock across an LLM call" (§5.3). Holding
  it would block every poll and every sibling category write.
  - Both the finalize and reaper terminal writes use
  filter(status="blue").update(...) — a compare-and-set. Whichever of the two
  runs first wins; the loser's updated == 0 raises SupersededGeneration and
  rolls back. That's how exactly one terminal writer is guaranteed.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Now Phase 4, Task 15: DRF serializers and endpoints. Update tracker, write API
  tests.

       22 +def exception_handler(exc, context):                          
       23 +    """Wrap every DRF error in the {detail, field} envelope (§
          +13)."""                                                       
       24 +    response = drf_exception_handler(exc, context)            
       25 +    if response is None:                                      
       26 +        return None                                           
       27 +    data = response.data                                      
       28 +    field, detail = None, None                                
       29 +    if isinstance(data, dict):                                
       30 +        if set(data.keys()) == {"detail"}:                    
       31 +            detail = str(data["detail"])                      
       32 +        else:                                                 
       33 +            first_field, msgs = next(iter(data.items()))      
       34 +            if isinstance(msgs, (list, tuple)):               
       35 +                detail = str(msgs[0])                         
       36 +            else:                                             
       37 +                detail = str(msgs)                            
       38 +            field = None if first_field == "non_field_errors" 
          +else first_field                                              
       39 +    elif isinstance(data, list):                              
       40 +        detail = str(data[0]) if data else "Invalid request." 
       41 +    response.data = {"detail": detail, "field": field}        
       42 +    return response                                           
       43 +                                                              
       44 +                                                              
       45 +def revoke_task_ids(task_ids):                                
       46 +    """Best-effort revoke of Celery task ids. Correctness reli
          +es on fencing,                                                
       47 +    not this (Section 5.7). Never terminate=True (would SIGTER
          +M a sibling)."""                                              
       48 +    from drumbeat.celery import app                           
       49 +    for task_id in task_ids or []:                            
       50 +        try:                                                  
       51 +            app.control.revoke(task_id)                       
       52 +        except Exception as exc:                              
       53 +            _log.warning("revoke failed: %s", exc)            
       54 +                                                              
       55 +                                                              
       56 +def _dispatch(run_id):                                        
       57 +    """Dispatch start_run and store its id (best-effort) at th
          +e current gen."""                                             
       58 +    res = start_run.delay(run_id)                             
       59 +    try:  # fenced idempotent append at the CURRENT generation
       60 +        gen = Run.objects.values_list("generation", flat=True)
          +.get(id=run_id)                                               
       61 +        append_celery_task_id(run_id, gen, str(res.id))       
       62 +    except (SupersededGeneration, Run.DoesNotExist):          
       63 +        pass  # a refresh/delete raced; id storage is best-eff
          +ort only                                                      
       64 +                                                              
       65 +                                                              
       66 +class RunListCreateView(APIView):                             
       67 +    def get(self, request):                                   
       68 +        default = int(os.environ.get("RUN_LIST_PAGE_SIZE", "50
          +"))                                                           
       69 +        try:                                                  
       70 +            limit = min(int(request.query_params.get("limit", 
          +default)), 200)                                               
       71 +            offset = int(request.query_params.get("offset", 0)
          +)                                                             
       72 +        except ValueError:                                    
       73 +            limit, offset = default, 0                        
       74 +        qs = Run.objects.all()  # Meta.ordering = ["-id"] -> n
          +ewest first                                                   
       75 +        count = qs.count()                                    
       76 +        rows = qs[offset:offset + limit]                      
       77 +        return Response({"results": RunListSerializer(rows, ma
          +ny=True).data,                                                
       78 +                         "count": count})                     
       79 +                                                              
       80 +    def post(self, request):                                  
       81 +        serializer = RunCreateSerializer(data=request.data)   
       82 +        serializer.is_valid(raise_exception=True)             
       83 +        with transaction.atomic():                            
       84 +            run = serializer.save()                           
       85 +            Run.objects.filter(id=run.id).update(started_at=ti
          +mezone.now())                                                 
       86 +            run.refresh_from_db()                             
       87 +            transaction.on_commit(lambda: _dispatch(run.id))  
       88 +        return Response(RunDetailSerializer(run).data,        
       89 +                        status=status.HTTP_201_CREATED)       
       90 +                                                              
       91 +                                                              
       92 +class RunDetailView(APIView):                                 
       93 +    def get(self, request, pk):                               
       94 +        run = _get_or_404(pk)                                 
       95 +        if run is None:                                       
       96 +            return _not_found()                               
       97 +        return Response(RunDetailSerializer(run).data)        
       98 +                                                              
       99 +    def delete(self, request, pk):                            
      100 +        run = _get_or_404(pk)                                 
      101 +        if run is None:                                       
      102 +            return _not_found()                               
      103 +        old_ids = list(run.celery_task_ids)                   
      104 +        run.delete()  # cascades to Category/ContentItem      
      105 +        revoke_task_ids(old_ids)  # best-effort, outside any t
          +ransaction                                                    
      106 +        return Response(status=status.HTTP_204_NO_CONTENT)    
      107 +                                                              
      108 +                                                              
      109 +class RunRefreshView(APIView):                                
      110 +    def post(self, request, pk):                              
      111 +        run = _get_or_404(pk)                                 
      112 +        if run is None:                                       
      113 +            return _not_found()                               
      114 +        old_ids = list(run.celery_task_ids)                   
      115 +        selected = list(run.selected_categories)              
      116 +        with transaction.atomic():                            
      117 +            bump_generation(run.id)                           
      118 +            run.categories.all().delete()  # cascades to items
      119 +            Run.objects.filter(id=run.id).update(             
      120 +                status="blue", started_at=timezone.now(), ende
          +d_at=None,                                                    
      121 +                executive_overview=None, error=None, warnings=
          +[],                                                           
      122 +                celery_task_ids=[])                           
      123 +            run.refresh_from_db()                             
      124 +            _create_categories(run, selected)                 
      125 +            transaction.on_commit(lambda: _dispatch(run.id))  
      126 +        revoke_task_ids(old_ids)  # best-effort, outside the t
          +ransaction                                                    
      127 +        return Response(RunDetailSerializer(run).data)        
      128 +                                                              
      129 +                                                              
      130 +def _get_or_404(pk):                                          
      131 +    return Run.objects.filter(id=pk).first()                  
      132 +                                                              
      133 +                                                              
      134 +def _not_found():                                             
      135 +    return Response({"detail": "Run not found.", "field": None
          +},                                                            
      136 +                    status=status.HTTP_404_NOT_FOUND)

● All green, environment clean, working tree committed. The implementation is

  Summary

  I implemented the full Drumbeat plan (plans/IMPLEMENTATION.md, all 22 tasks)
  via strict TDD, committing each task. Then I ran three adversarial review
  rounds with fresh-context agents, looping until no significant feedback
  remained.

  What was built (Django + DRF API, Celery-over-Redis research fan-out,
  SQLite/WAL, React+Vite frontend):

  - Core primitives first (per §22 ordering): category registry, the shared
  URL/domain module (tldextract, offline), own-channel + denylist exclusion, the
  data model, the completeness status model, and the generation-fencing write
  helper — including the proven rowcount-abort that rolls back child inserts
  against a superseded run.
  - External seams: call_llm() with per-call-point env resolution + fallback +
  redacted per-PID rotating LLM log; strict fail-loud structured-output parsers;
  tavily_search() with the two-layer time window.
  - Orchestration: the Celery chord (IDENTITY → per-category subtasks → fan-in),
  the bounded agentic curator loop, cross-category dedup computed outside any
  transaction, and the reaper — all generation-fenced with the three sanctioned
  broad-except boundaries.
  - API: serializers matching the §11 contract, the {detail, field} error
  envelope, every 400 validation path, and concurrency-safe refresh/delete.
  - Frontend: visibility-aware polling with backoff, home/run-list/new-run modal
  (focus-trapped), and the run-view with collapsible categories, accessible
  status chips, and explicit empty/error states.
  - Ops: start_all.sh (dynamic ports, run-scoped Redis, readiness wait, worker
  supervision, process-group teardown) and run_tests.sh (aggregated gate).

  Verification: ./run_tests.sh is green — 115 backend + 24 frontend tests. I
  also booted a real Celery worker against a throwaway Redis: it registers all
  four tasks, runs embedded beat, and fires the reaper on schedule.

  Review outcomes: the concurrency/fencing core, API contract, config, and
  frontend were all confirmed free of critical/major bugs. Fixes applied:
  start_all.sh process-group cleanup (was leaking worker/vite/runserver
  grandchildren) and port-collision guard; LLM-log propagation off in
  production; .NET-style name classification; ingest cap guard; serializer N+1
  removed via prefetch; and two frontend a11y/error-surfacing fixes.

  One honest caveat: Task 22's full manual end-to-end run against a real company
  (streaming categories, executive overview, own-domain exclusion in the live
  UI) needs real LLM + Tavily keys in .env, so I marked it PARTIAL — the
  automated portions (worker boot, task registration, migrations, script
  teardown, both suites) are verified; the human-in-the-loop run against a live
  company is left for you to do once keys are in place with ./start_all.sh.

      38 +def is_safe_http_url(url):                                     
      39 +    """True only for http(s) URLs with a hostname.             
      40 +                                                               
      41 +    Blocks javascript:, data:, file:, mailto:, and protocol-rel
         +ative URLs so an                                               
      42 +    external search result can never become an unsafe clickable
         + link.                                                         
      43 +    """                                                        
      44 +    if not isinstance(url, str):                               
      45 +        return False                                           
      46 +    try:                                                       
      47 +        parts = urlsplit(url.strip())                          
      48 +    except ValueError:                                         
      49 +        return False                                           
      50 +    return parts.scheme in ("http", "https") and bool(parts.hos
         +tname)                                                         
      51 +                                                               
      52 +

      54      """Return the public-suffix registrable domain, or None."""
      55      ext = _extract(_with_scheme(host_or_url))

      133 +def _query_int(request, name, default, minimum):              
      134 +    """Parse an integer query param or raise a 400 (name-tagge
          +d) envelope."""                                               
      135 +    raw = request.query_params.get(name)                      
      136 +    if raw is None:                                           
      137 +        return default                                        
      138 +    try:                                                      
      139 +        value = int(raw)                                      
      140 +    except (TypeError, ValueError):                           
      141 +        raise ValidationError({name: f"{name} must be an integ
          +er."})                                                        
      142 +    if value < minimum:                                       
      143 +        raise ValidationError({name: f"{name} must be >= {mini
          +mum}."})                                                      
      144 +    return value                                              
      145 +

  What I changed

  #: 1
  Finding: High — tldextract wrote to the home cache, failing the gate on a
  read-only home
  Fix: TLDExtract(suffix_list_urls=(), cache_dir=None) — fully offline via the
  bundled snapshot, no disk writes. Regression test asserts _cache.enabled  is 
  False.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Finding: High — external URLs rendered as links without scheme validation
  Fix: Added is_safe_http_url() to the shared URL module; tavily_search drops
  non-http(s) results; ContentItemRow renders the title as plain text (not a
  link) if the scheme is unsafe.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Finding: Medium — borderline_options truthy-string coercion
  ({"reddit":"false"}
  enabled Reddit)
  Fix: Serializer now rejects any non-boolean value with a 400 field error.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Finding: Medium — run-list pagination silently fell back on bad input
  Fix: _query_int parses limit/offset, returning a 400 envelope for
  non-integers,
  negatives, and non-positive limits.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Finding: Low/Med — LLM log omitted tool-call content
  Fix: Log payload now records response as {content, tool_calls} (redaction by
  omission preserved).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Finding: Low — README npm install ran in the wrong directory
  Fix: Changed to (cd frontend && npm install).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Finding: Low — one frontend line > 94 chars
  Fix: Wrapped the stale banner in RunView.jsx.

❯ A few improvements:                                                           
                                                                                
  1. When I'm in the run-view, I should be able to see a link back to the home  
  page.                                                                         
  2. Various errors are displayed to the user for different categories (I tried 
  two runs, and they are not always the same):                                  
     - Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0) You can retry via Refresh.     
     - Unterminated string starting at: line 153 column 14 (char 9917) You can  
  retry via Refresh.                                                            
     - accepted must be a list of {url: str} You can retry via Refresh.         
  2.1. These errors look like unhandled variances in the LLM responses. We      
  might consider using Instructor to get data back in a specific form.          
  2.2. Secondly, those errors should not be displayed to the user. The user     
  should just see a more generic indication that an error occurred. However,    
  the Django logs did not show any error!                                       
  3. By default, the sections/categories should be collapsed.                   
  4. The "Partial" badge should show something like "3 of 5", or similar, to    
  indicate how many sections were successfully handled.

  the vite server. It should always try given (non standard) port, and only if  
  that one is not available, try another one, ideally also not randomly         
  chosen. That way, I don't have to redo the copy-and-paste for the FE after    
  every restart.

● All five improvements are implemented, tested (129 backend + 29 frontend, gate
  exit 0), and committed, with the design docs updated to match.

  What changed

  1. Home link in the run-view — added a "← All runs" link at the top of the
  run-view (both the loading and loaded states), linking to /.

  2. Category errors — generic to the user, logged for you, and more robust
  - The "no logs" bug is fixed. The subtask boundary caught the exception and
  stored str(exc) into Category.error but never logged it. Now
  _mark_category_red logs the real error with full traceback to the drumbeat
  application log (and so do the fan-in degrade and non-fatal IDENTITY paths).
  Django logs will now show these.
  - The user sees a generic message, not the raw parser output. Category.error
  now stores only "This category could not be researched due to an error.", and
  the frontend renders a fixed generic line — so Expecting value… / Unterminated
  string… / accepted must be a list… never reach the UI.
  - 2.1 robustness — rather than adding the Instructor dependency, I enabled the
  OpenAI-compatible JSON mode (response_format={"type":"json_object"}) on all
  structured call-points (IDENTITY, QUERY_PLANNER, CURATOR, CATEGORY_SUMMARY,
  REPORT), toggleable via LLM_JSON_MODE. This removes the "Expecting value"
  (non-JSON) and shape failures. For the "Unterminated string at char 9917" (a
  max-tokens truncation), I made the curator's rejected/duplicates fields
  optional (they were required but unused) and told the model to return only the
  accepted URLs — a much smaller payload that won't truncate. Instructor /
  provider json_schema mode is recorded in docs/FUTURE-IMPROVEMENTS.md as the
  next step if JSON mode proves insufficient.

  3. Categories collapsed by default — all sections start collapsed on first
  load; per-section toggles are still remembered for the session.

  4. "Partial" badge shows scope — a yellow run now reads "Partial (N of M)",
  where N = categories that finished without error and M = total. (The home
  list, which has no per-category data, still shows plain "Partial".)

  5. Deterministic ports — start_all.sh no longer picks random ports. Each
  service prefers a fixed non-standard port (Redis 6390, Django 8390, Vite 5390)
  and only advances to the next sequential free port if it's busy. The Vite URL
  stays stable across restarts, and it now prints Open the app at: 
  http://localhost:5390. Overridable via DRUMBEAT_{REDIS,DJANGO,VITE}_PORT. I
  verified the picker behavior (preferred-first, sequential fallback,
  collision-exclusion) in isolation.

  One decision worth flagging: for 2.1 I chose native JSON mode over Instructor
  because it needs no new dependency and works with the plan's target providers
  (OpenAI/OpenRouter). If your provider rejects response_format, set
  LLM_JSON_MODE=0. If you'd specifically like me to wire in Instructor (or
  provider json_schema structured outputs with a bounded retry), I can do that
  as a follow-up — it's the stronger guarantee.

● Now I understand the root cause. IDENTITY is failing on real OpenRouter output
  because the model returns:
  - "confidence": 0.9 (a number, not the enum "high"/"medium"/"low")
  - "matched": ["https://..."] (an array, not a boolean)

  These are non-fatal (the run proceeds), but the strict parser throws away a
  perfectly good official_domain: "apple.com" over the unused confidence field —
  so own-domain exclusion is silently skipped for Apple/Google. JSON mode
  guarantees valid JSON but not these value shapes; the fix is a well-specified
  prompt plus a tolerant IDENTITY parser that salvages the domain (IDENTITY is
  best-effort by design). Let me look at the current parser and prompt.

● Fixed. The Celery-log errors were all from the IDENTITY step, and they were
  the only errors — every research subtask, the fan-in, and the reaper
  succeeded, so your runs completed; the damage was silent.

  What was actually happening

  The three log lines were:
  identity resolution failed for run 1: confidence must be high/medium/low
  identity resolution failed for run 2: matched must be bool
  Looking at the actual OpenRouter responses in the LLM log, the model returned
  valid JSON with the right domain, but:
  - "confidence": 0.9 — a number, not the enum "high"/"medium"/"low"
  - "matched": ["https://blog.google/…"] — an array of evidence, not a boolean

  The strict parser rejected the whole object over those two fields and threw
  away a perfectly good official_domain: "apple.com" / "google.com". Since
  IDENTITY is non-fatal, the run continued — but own-channel exclusion was 
  silently skipped, so Apple's/Google's own newsroom and blog pages would not be
  filtered out of their own results. (JSON mode guarantees valid JSON but can't
  constrain these value shapes; only the prompt/parser can.)

  The fix

  1. Tolerant IDENTITY parser — IDENTITY is best-effort by design, and its only
  load-bearing output is the domain, so the parser now salvages it: numeric
  confidence is normalised to the enum, an array/other matched is derived from
  whether a real domain was found, and owned_profile_urls/handles are filtered
  to strings. Only a genuinely non-JSON body still fails. The other call-points
  (QUERY_PLANNER/CURATOR/CATEGORY_SUMMARY/REPORT) stay strict — they gate
  category/run status.
  2. Explicit IDENTITY prompt — now states each field's exact type (domain as a
  bare string, confidence ∈ high/medium/low, matched as a boolean) with a
  one-shot example, so the model produces the right shapes in the first place.

  I verified the fix against the exact responses from your logs: both apple.com
  and google.com now resolve with matched: true → own-domain exclusion will run.
  Full suite: 131 backend + 29 frontend passing. The plan §6.1 records the
  tolerant-IDENTITY decision.

  One thing worth noting for your next run: because IDENTITY was failing, those
  earlier runs did not exclude the company's own domain — a fresh run (or
  Refresh) will now apply the exclusion correctly.  ⎿  Stop says: ✦ 9 memories woven into the palace — plan, user, messages

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Diagnosis complete and fixed.

  Why Google had no blogs

  It wasn't a bug in exclusion or the curator — both worked correctly. The
  QUERY_PLANNER was the culprit. For Google's blog_posts category it generated
  queries like:

  "latest Google blog posts", "Google AI blog articles", "Google Cloud blog
  updates" …

  Every one of those searches for Google's own blogs, so Tavily returned
  blog.google, opensource.googleblog.com, research.google,
  mapsplatform.google.com, etc. The curator then correctly rejected all of them
  as company_channel — because that category means third-party blog posts (§2).
  Result: 0 accepted → yellow.

  The planner prompt only passed the bare category key (Category: blog_posts)
  and never said we want independent, third-party coverage about the company
  rather than the company's own content. (Apple got 9 blogs because the
  third-party Apple-blog ecosystem — 9to5Mac and friends — is large enough that
  even naive queries surfaced some; Google's own blogs dominate its blog
  searches.)

  The fix

  1. Third-party-targeted planner — the QUERY_PLANNER prompt now states it's
  finding independent, third-party content about the company, carries a
  per-category intent (e.g. blog_posts → "blog posts by INDEPENDENT third
  parties — NOT the company's own blog"), and injects the resolved own-domain as
  an explicit "do not target google.com" hint. Verified the rendered Google
  prompt now steers correctly.
  2. Log correlation — this was hard to trace because the pipeline's call_llm
  calls (planner/curator/summary) logged run_id=None/category_key=None; only
  IDENTITY/REPORT were tagged. I threaded run_id/category_key through so every
  LLM record is now correlated, per §7.

  Full suite: 132 backend + 29 frontend passing. The plan records the
  third-party-planner requirement.

  A couple of honest caveats:
  - There's a deeper related limitation still deferred
  (docs/FUTURE-IMPROVEMENTS.md): tavily_search hardcodes topic="news" for all
  categories, so blog/podcast/newsletter searches run through the news index.
  The prompt fix mitigates the own-channel problem, but per-category Tavily
  topic/params tuning would improve blog/podcast recall further — say the word
  and I'll implement it.
  - The runs already in your DB won't change retroactively; a fresh run (or
  Refresh) of Google will use the improved planner.
  ⎿  Stop says: ✦ 10 memories woven into the palace — plan, user, messages

❯ Can we create a 'sentiment graph', where we analyse what we find and capture  
  the sentiment of whatever article or piece of information, and then plot this 
  on a graph, so that the user can see the general sentiment development over   
  the 36 months (or whatever) period?                                           

● This is a new feature — sentiment analysis per item plus a time-series graph.
  Before designing or writing code, let me use the brainstorming skill to
  explore the requirements and design space with you.

● Skill(superpowers:brainstorming)
  ⎿  Successfully loaded skill

● I've built this codebase, so I have full context. Let me work through the
  design with a few focused questions, starting with the most consequential one.

  The project has a deliberately cost-conscious, "boring stack" ethos — the plan
  even deferred per-item LLM summaries for cost. Sentiment scoring has the same
  tension, so how we compute it is the biggest design decision.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · How should we compute the sentiment of each item, given the cost/accuracy
     trade-off? → One LLM call per item

● Understood — per-item scoring it is. That gives each item its own sentiment
  with full context (I'll bound it with the existing per-category item cap so
  cost stays predictable).

  Next: how should each item's sentiment be represented? This drives what the
  graph can show.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · How should each item's sentiment be represented (and therefore what the 
     graph plots)? → Numeric −1..+1 + label

● Numeric score with a label — that makes a clean trend line and colored item
  rows.

  Now, what should the graph itself show over the lookback window?

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · What should the sentiment graph display over the lookback window? → 
     Monthly average line (run-level)
● Run-level monthly average line with volume — clear and focused.

  One more real choice before I lay out the full design: the frontend currently
  has no charting library. For a monthly line chart we can either add a small
  dependency or hand-roll it.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · How should we render the line chart, given the project's 
     minimal-dependency ethos? → Add Recharts

● I have what I need. Here's the proposed design — please tell me if any section
  needs changing.

  Sentiment graph — design

  1. Data model — add two nullable fields to ContentItem:
  - sentiment_score — float in [−1.0, +1.0], null when unknown/unscored
  - sentiment_label — "positive" | "neutral" | "negative", null when unknown

  Migration 0002. Nullable because sentiment is best-effort (below).

  2. New SENTIMENT LLM call-point (per item, non-fatal)
  - Sixth call-point with SENTIMENT_LLM_* env overrides → DEFAULT_LLM_*, and a
  SENTIMENT_ENABLED toggle (default on) so the extra per-item cost can be
  switched off.
  - schemas.parse_sentiment → {score, label}; tolerant like IDENTITY (clamp
  score to [−1,1]; derive label from score if missing). Prompt gets the item
  title + snippet + company; JSON mode on.
  - Best-effort / non-fatal, mirroring IDENTITY: a failed or malformed score →
  that item's sentiment stays null (logged server-side), the item is still kept,
  and the category is never turned red. Run/category status logic is unchanged
  — sentiment doesn't affect completeness.

  3. Pipeline — after curation + the item cap, research_category scores each
  kept item's sentiment and attaches sentiment_score/sentiment_label to the item
  dict; the subtask persists them. Bounded by a new SENTIMENT_MAX_ITEMS
  (default = MAX_ITEMS_PER_CATEGORY, ≤20). Note: per-item scoring is sequential
  inside the subtask, so a busy category gains latency — I'll size it against
  the existing soft_time_limit and make the cap tunable.

  4. Aggregation (server-side, per §10/§11 "frontend never counts") —
  RunDetailSerializer gains:
  - sentiment_timeline: one bucket per calendar month across the run's lookback
  window, [{month, avg_score, item_count}], computed from dated, scored items
  (empty months carry avg_score: null so the x-axis is continuous).
  - sentiment_summary: {overall_avg, scored_count, undated_scored_count, 
  unknown_count} — a headline figure plus honest coverage (undated items are
  scored but can't be placed in time, so they're excluded from the line and
  reported separately).
  - ContentItemSerializer gains sentiment_score, sentiment_label.

  5. Frontend (Recharts) — a SentimentGraph in the run-view (below the executive
  overview): responsive line chart, y from −1…+1, a reference line at 0,
  tooltip with month/avg/count, colored by sign. Shows an empty state when there
  are <2 dated+scored points. Each ContentItemRow gets a small colored
  sentiment pill (label + score, or "—" when unknown). A one-line note reports
  any undated-but-scored items.

  6. Testing — parser (valid/clamped/derived/malformed); pipeline scoring
  (attaches scores, failed item → null, respects cap); serializer bucketing
  (dated-only, undated excluded, empty case, overall_avg); frontend (graph
  renders points + empty state, item chip for positive/negative/unknown).

  Scope guardrails (YAGNI): run-level line only (no per-category toggle), no
  per-item sentiment rationale stored, no re-scoring on refresh beyond the
  normal re-run.

  Does this look right, or would you adjust anything — e.g. the empty-month
  handling, the SENTIMENT_ENABLED/cap defaults, or where the graph sits in the
  page?

         -le.                                                            
      49 +  further bounded by `SENTIMENT_MAX_ITEMS` (**default 10**, cho
         +sen so the worst                                               
      50 +  case fits comfortably under `task_soft_time_limit`; NOT the f
         +ull                                                            
      51 +  `MAX_ITEMS_PER_CATEGORY` of 20). A global `SENTIMENT_ENABLED`

         +ential inside the

         +me_limit`.

  Review outcome

  Three rounds, converged. The plan (plans/SENTIMENT-IMPLEMENTATION.md) and
  design (plans/SENTIMENT-DESIGN.md) are now ready for implementation.

  Round 1 (3 parallel agents) — found 3 MAJOR issues plus doc/process gaps, all
  fixed:
  - recharts + jsdom: ResponsiveContainer needs ResizeObserver, which jsdom
  lacks — the charted test would have thrown. Added a ResizeObserver stub to
  setupTests.js.
  - SoftTimeLimitExceeded swallow: score_sentiments' broad except Exception
  would have caught Celery's soft-limit signal (it's an Exception subclass),
  converting a clean 180s red into a 210s hard-kill/stuck-blue. Now re-raises
  it; lowered SENTIMENT_MAX_ITEMS default to 10 to keep the sequential per-item
  calls under the soft limit.
  - Design-contract violation: the graph would have shown its empty-state box on
  every in-progress run. Now gated on scored_count > 0 (hidden while blue/no
  data), with headline + coverage notes always shown.
  - Plus: unknown_count note rendered, data[key] over .get() (CLAUDE.md), all
  three call-point enumerations + the broad-except invariant updated together,
  and deferred items recorded in docs/FUTURE-IMPROVEMENTS.md.

  Round 2 (2 fresh agents) — 6/7 fixes verified correct; found 1 MAJOR (a test
  using getByText("News articles") that matches two elements) and 2 minors
  (connectNulls vs the design's "gaps" wording; a missing subtask-level failure
  test). All fixed.

  Round 3 (final agent) — all round-2 fixes CORRECT, no new critical/major 
  issues, verdict: ready for implementation. One cosmetic wording nit fixed.

  The plan is 8 tasks, TDD throughout, each with complete code and an
  independently-testable deliverable, and it preserves every base-app invariant
  (non-fatal sentiment, generation-fenced writes, server-side aggregation, no
  txn across LLM calls, status logic untouched).

  Would you like me to proceed to implementation, or would you like to review
  the revised plan first?

      136 +    snip = int(os.environ.get("MAX_SNIPPET_CHARS", "300"))    
      137 +    return (

      144 +def score_sentiments(company, items, run_id=None, category_key
          +=None):                                                       
      145 +    """Attach sentiment_score/label to each item (best-effort,
          + non-fatal).                                                  
      146 +                                                              
      147 +    One LLM call per item, bounded by SENTIMENT_MAX_ITEMS. A p
          +er-item failure or                                            
      148 +    a disabled feature leaves that item's sentiment None; it n
          +ever raises and                                               
      149 +    never affects category status (§5.4).                     
      150 +    """                                                       
      151 +    enabled = os.environ.get("SENTIMENT_ENABLED", "1") != "0" 
      152 +    cap = int(os.environ.get("SENTIMENT_MAX_ITEMS", "10"))    
      153 +    for idx, item in enumerate(items):                        
      154 +        item["sentiment_score"] = None                        
      155 +        item["sentiment_label"] = None                        
      156 +        if not enabled or idx >= cap:                         
      157 +            continue                                          
      158 +        try:                                                  
      159 +            out = call_llm(                                   
      160 +                "SENTIMENT",                                  
      161 +                [{"role": "user", "content": _sentiment_prompt
          +(company, item)}],                                            
      162 +                json_object=True, run_id=run_id, category_key=
          +category_key)                                                 
      163 +            data = schemas.parse_sentiment(out["content"])    
      164 +            item["sentiment_score"] = data["score"]           
      165 +            item["sentiment_label"] = data["label"]           
      166 +        except SoftTimeLimitExceeded:                         
      167 +            # SoftTimeLimitExceeded IS an Exception; do NOT sw
          +allow it, or the                                              
      168 +            # 180s soft limit is lost and the task grinds to t
          +he 210s hard                                                  
      169 +            # SIGKILL (stuck-blue). Let it propagate to the su
          +btask boundary,                                               
      170 +            # which marks the category red cleanly (§5.4/§5.5)
          +.                                                             
      171 +            raise                                             
      172 +        except Exception as exc:  # non-fatal: unknown sentime
          +nt, keep the item                                             
      173 +            _log.warning("sentiment scoring failed for %s: %s"
          +,                                                             
      174 +                         item.get("url"), exc)                
      175 +    return items

       36 +def _iter_months(start_ym, end_ym):                           
       37 +    """Yield (year, month) inclusive from start_ym to end_ym (
          +both (y, m))."""                                              
       38 +    y, m = start_ym                                           
       39 +    while (y, m) <= end_ym:                                   
       40 +        yield y, m                                            
       41 +        m = 1 if m == 12 else m + 1                           
       42 +        y = y + 1 if m == 1 else y                            
       43 +                                                              
       44 +                                                              
       45 +def _window_start(ref, lookback_months):                      
       46 +    """First (year, month) of a lookback_months window ending 
          +in ref's month."""                                            
       47 +    index = ref.year * 12 + (ref.month - 1) - (lookback_months
          + - 1)                                                         
       48 +    return index // 12, index % 12 + 1                        
       49 +                                                              
       50 +

● The sentiment-graph feature is fully implemented, and the adversarial
  code-review loop has converged.

  Summary

  All 8 tasks from plans/SENTIMENT-IMPLEMENTATION.md are complete and committed,
  followed by two code-review rounds.

  What was built:
  - parse_sentiment — a tolerant parser (clamps score to [−1,1], derives the
  label from the score, treats unusable/non-object output as unknown).
  - ContentItem.sentiment_score / sentiment_label — two nullable fields +
  migration 0002.
  - score_sentiments — one LLM call per kept item through a new non-fatal
  SENTIMENT call-point, bounded by SENTIMENT_MAX_ITEMS (default 10), toggleable
  via SENTIMENT_ENABLED. It re-raises SoftTimeLimitExceeded (so a timeout still
  marks the category red cleanly) but swallows every other per-item failure to a
  null score — a category is never turned red by sentiment.
  - Server-side aggregation — RunDetailSerializer gains sentiment_timeline
  (continuous monthly buckets over the lookback window, dated+scored items only)
  and sentiment_summary (overall average + honest coverage counts), computed
  off the existing prefetch (no N+1).
  - Frontend — a Recharts SentimentGraph (mounted only when there's scored data,
  per §6), a per-item colored sentiment pill with a text label (WCAG 1.4.1),
  and the ResizeObserver shim for jsdom.

  Verification: ./run_tests.sh is green — 153 backend + 36 frontend tests, plus

● Fixed. Here's what was wrong and what it means for you.

  Root cause

  Your Apple run had all 60 items undated, so the sentiment graph had nothing to
  plot — but the items did have dates. Tavily returns published_date in RFC 
  2822 format ("Thu, 12 Feb 2026 21:57:14 GMT"), while our parser only
  understood ISO ("2026-02-12"). It took value[:10] = "Thu, 12 Fe", failed to
  parse it, and silently returned None for every single item.

  This is a pre-existing base-app bug that the sentiment feature happened to
  expose. The tests never caught it because the tavily_search mock seam was fed
  ISO-format dates in tests, so the real RFC 2822 format was never exercised.

  The fix

  _parse_date now parses RFC 2822 first (via email.utils.parsedate_to_datetime),
  falls back to ISO, and normalises to tz-aware UTC. Verified against your
  actual Tavily output — the dates now parse. Added a time-independent unit test
  plus an RFC 2822 regression test. Full suite green (155 backend + 36 
  frontend).

  Two things worth knowing

  1. Re-run Apple to see the graph. Your existing run's items are stored undated
  in the DB; the fix only affects new fetches. Hit Refresh on the run (or start
  a new one) and the timeline should populate — for Apple you'll get a real
  monthly sentiment trend.
  2. This also fixes time-window enforcement (§9), app-wide. Because every date
  was becoming None, the "drop items older than the lookback window" filter
  never dropped anything — out-of-window results were being kept too. Now that
  dates parse, a re-run will correctly exclude items older than the window, so
  per-category counts may drop somewhat (that's the intended, correct
  behaviour). The 11 items could not be scored is a separate, expected thing —
  those are per-item sentiment calls that failed and are non-fatal (they show up
  honestly in the "could not be scored" note).

  One caveat I should flag: even with this fix, some items will still be
  genuinely undated (social posts, some blogs, sources Tavily has no date for) —
  those are scored and counted but stay off the timeline by design, so you'll
  still see an "undated items" note, just a much smaller one.

❯ Two runs for different companies, and both times, the chart only went back    
  about a year, but not more. Why?                                              

● The chart line only covers where there's dated data, so this points at what
  Tavily actually returns for older content. Let me probe Tavily directly with a
  wide window and check the date spread.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed empirically — this is a Tavily limitation, not a bug in our code. I
  queried Tavily's news index for "Apple" at three window sizes:

  ┌──────────────┬────────────────────────┬─────────┐
  │ days passed  │ oldest result returned │ newest  │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ 365 (1 yr)   │ 2025-09-09             │ 2026-07 │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ 1080 (36 mo) │ 2025-10-10             │ 2026-07 │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ 3000 (~8 yr) │ 2025-10-10             │ 2026-07 │
  └──────────────┴────────────────────────┴─────────┘

  Even asking for 8 years back, Tavily returns nothing older than ~Oct 2025 (~10
  months). The days parameter has no effect beyond roughly a year. So the
  chart's line stops ~1 year back because that's the entire depth of Tavily's 
  news index — the older months exist on the x-axis but have no data.

  Why this happens

  We hardcode topic="news" for every category. That's deliberate, because of a
  Tavily quirk: only topic="news" returns a published_date at all, and its days
  time-window only applies under that topic. But the news index is strongly
  recency-biased — it just doesn't hold articles much older than a year. This
  exact limitation is already noted in docs/FUTURE-IMPROVEMENTS.md.

  The trade-off is baked in:
  - topic="news" → items have dates, but only ~1 year deep.
  - topic="general" → can surface older content, but Tavily returns no
  published_date, so everything comes back undated (right back to the
  empty-timeline problem).

  So there's no single Tavily setting that gives both dates and multi-year
  depth.

  If you want deeper history

  The realistic path is to stop relying on Tavily for dates and derive them
  ourselves:

  1. URL-path date extraction (smaller change): many article URLs embed the date
  — nypost.com/2026/02/18/…, forbes.com/…/2026/06/09/…. We parse that as a
  fallback. This mainly reduces the undated count on news results; on its own it
  won't extend past a year because Tavily still won't return older news.
  2. topic="general" + URL/content date extraction (bigger change): switch
  categories to the general index (which can reach older content), then date
  items from their URLs (and possibly page content). This is what actually
  unlocks multi-year history — at the cost of a noisier, less news-focused
  result set, and items whose URL has no date staying undated.

  Both are design changes with real trade-offs, so I'd rather not just apply one
  silently.

  Which would you like?
  - (a) Add URL-path date extraction only (quick win: fewer undated items,
  history still ~1 year), or
  - (b) Go for real multi-year depth via topic="general" + date extraction (more
  work, noisier results), or
  - (c) Leave it as-is — accept that the sentiment trend is a rolling ~12-month
  view, and I'll just make the chart's x-axis match the data (only show months
  that have data) so it doesn't look truncated.

  My honest recommendation is (a) now (cheap, strictly better) and consider (b)
  later if the ~1-year window proves too shallow — but it's your call.

● Done. Here's the honest picture.

  What I found and changed

  The days parameter was capping Tavily's news index at ~1 year — and
  start_date/end_date doesn't have that cap (my probe reached back to Jan 2024
  for a 3-year window). Critically, topic="general" (literal option b) returns
  no dates at all, which would have recreated the empty-timeline bug. So I
  pivoted to what actually serves your goal:

  1. News search now uses an explicit start_date/end_date window derived from
  your lookback, instead of days. This removes the hard ~1-year ceiling and
  keeps the publish dates the chart needs.
  2. URL-path date fallback — items Tavily can't date but whose URL embeds one
  (/2026/02/18/…) now get dated too, so fewer land in the "undated" bucket.

  Both are committed; full suite green (157 backend + 36 frontend), tested
  including URL-date edge cases (id-numbers and bad months are rejected).

  The honest caveat

  The fix makes older articles reachable, but Tavily ranks results by relevance,
  which skews recent. With the default 10 results per query, a typical search
  still returns mostly recent items, so the trend will extend further back than
  the flat ~12 months you saw — but it won't evenly fill 36 months. How far back
  it reaches depends on the query and how many results you pull. If you want
  deeper coverage, raise TAVILY_RESULTS_PER_SEARCH in your .env (e.g. to 20) —
  more results per query reach further back, at the cost of more
  curator/sentiment LLM calls.

  This is a genuine external-data limit: Tavily's news index is recency-weighted
  and simply thins out the further back you go. There's no setting that
  guarantees a dense multi-year history.

  To see it

  Same as before — restart ./start_all.sh so the worker loads the new tavily.py
  (Ctrl-C, then re-run), then start a fresh run. You should see the trend reach
  back further than a year, with fewer undated items.

  If after a clean restart the depth still feels too shallow for what you need,
  tell me and I'll wire per-category result counts or targeted historical
  queries — but I'd rather see the real result first than add complexity
  speculatively.

❯ Ok, now the limit seems to be 2 years (confirmed with two runs for different  
  companies).                                                                   
● ~2 years is a real improvement (from ~1). The question now is whether that's a
  hard wall in Tavily's index or something we can push further. Let me probe
  directly — can Tavily's news index return anything older than 2 years, even
  for queries explicitly about old events?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● That result is the real insight — 2 years is not Tavily's index depth; it's 
  relevance-crowding. When I explicitly target old date ranges, Tavily happily
  returns much older content:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │          Query + date range          │       returned        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ "Apple WWDC 2022" [2022]             │ June 2022 (4 yrs old) │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ "Apple iPhone 12 launch 2020" [2020] │ Oct 2020 (6 yrs old)  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ "Apple news 2023" [2023]             │ Apr–Dec 2023          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  So the old articles are in Tavily's index. The problem: when you ask for one
  wide 3-year window, Tavily returns the 10 most relevant results, and recent
  articles almost always outrank older ones — so the old ones exist but never
  make the top 10. Ask for a narrow old window and they surface immediately.

  The fix, and its cost

  To get a dense multi-year timeline, search in time buckets instead of one wide
  window: split the lookback into sub-windows (e.g. one per year) and run each
  query once per bucket, so each period returns its own results and older ones
  aren't crowded out.

  The catch is cost — it multiplies the number of Tavily searches (and therefore
  downstream curator + per-item sentiment LLM calls) by the number of buckets:

  - Yearly buckets over 36 months ≈ 3× the searches/LLM cost.
  - 6-month buckets ≈ 6×.

  Right now a run already does queries × categories searches; bucketing
  multiplies that. For a 7-category run that's a real increase in time and API
  spend.

  This is a genuine cost/coverage trade-off that's yours to make. Which would
  you like?

  - (a) Implement yearly time-bucketing (≈3× cost for a 36-month run) — dense
  multi-year trend.
  - (b) Make the bucket size a config knob (default off / 1 bucket = current
  behavior; set e.g. SEARCH_TIME_BUCKETS=yearly when you want depth) — you
  control the cost per run.
  - (c) Leave it — accept that the trend is dense for ~2 recent years and
  sparse/absent before that.

  My recommendation is (b): it defaults to today's behavior (no extra cost), but
  lets you flip on deeper history when you want it. Your call.

● Implemented exactly your design.

  What it does now

  tavily_search splits the lookback into time buckets:
  - ≤ 12-month lookback → one search (recent window), no extra cost.
  - > 12-month lookback → two searches per query: a recent-12-month bucket and
  one bucket for the entire remaining range (12 months → lookback). Results are
  deduped by canonical URL across the two buckets.

  So it's ~2× the search/LLM cost only for multi-year runs — the older bucket
  forces Tavily to return older articles that its recency ranking otherwise
  buries.

  Verified

  - The same "Apple Vision Pro" query that previously stopped at 2025-11 now
  reaches 2025-01 — the older bucket is contributing.
  - Tests: added _time_buckets coverage and a two-bucket dedup test; full suite
  green (159 backend + 36 frontend), no long lines.

  Honest caveat

  This meaningfully widens the spread, but it's not a guarantee of an
  evenly-filled 36 months. Within the older bucket Tavily still ranks by
  relevance, so it front-loads the most-relevant older items (often 2024–2025),
  not necessarily the very oldest. Aggregated across ~3 planner queries × 7
  categories × 2 buckets, the trend will be much denser across the last ~2–3
  years, but it may still thin toward the far end — the exact reach is
  query-dependent. If you later want it denser still, the knob is more buckets
  (e.g. yearly) or a higher TAVILY_RESULTS_PER_SEARCH, both at more cost.

  To see it

  Restart ./start_all.sh so the worker loads the new tavily.py, then start a
  fresh run. You should see the trend reach back further and more evenly than
  the 2-year wall you just hit.

  Here's the design I'd build; tell me if it matches what you want:

  Design

  A mode selector (small segmented control / radio) in the SentimentGraph
  header:
  - "Monthly average" — the current line chart (unchanged default).
  - "Individual items" — a scatter plot.

  Individual-items mode (Recharts ComposedChart on a shared time x-axis):
  - One dot per dated, scored item, positioned at (published_at, 
  sentiment_score), colored by sentiment sign (green / grey / red) — so
  positive/negative outliers pop visually.
  - The monthly-average line overlaid through the dots (reusing the existing
  sentiment_timeline), so you see both the individual spread and the trend.
  - Hover a dot → a tooltip with the item's title, source, date, score, and 
  snippet (the summary text we already store), and I'll make the title a bit
  prominent so you can identify the outlier. (Optionally the dot links out on
  click — say if you want that.)

  Scope / behavior:
  - Data is derived on the frontend from items already present — zero backend or
  LLM cost.
  - Undated items still can't be placed (no x-position), consistent with the
  line; the "N undated" note stays.
  - The graph still only appears when there's scored data; the mode toggle
  persists per session.
  - The number of dots is bounded by the existing caps (~10 scored
  items/category), so it won't be overwhelming.

  monthly-average line (same series as today's chart). Is that what you mean by
  "an average graph through it," or did you want a smoothed regression/trend

● Built and committed.

  What you now have

  A mode selector on the sentiment graph:
  - "Monthly average" (default) — the existing trend line, unchanged.
  - "Individual items" — a scatter plot where each dot is one article/item at
  (publish date, sentiment score), colored by sentiment (green / grey / red),
  with the monthly-average line overlaid through them so you see both the spread
  and the trend.

  In items mode:
  - Hover a dot → a tooltip with the item's title, source, date, score, and 
  snippet — so you can identify what's driving a positive or negative outlier.
  - Click a dot → opens that article in a new tab (noopener,noreferrer).
  - The mode choice persists for the session (sessionStorage).

  It reuses the item data already in the run payload, so it's frontend-only — no
  backend or LLM cost. Full suite green (159 backend + 39 frontend), build
  clean.

  To see it

  This is a frontend-only change, so no worker restart is needed — Vite's
  hot-reload will pick it up. Just reload the run-view page in your browser and

● Done. Here's the reasoning and what I changed.

  Why it happens

  The pipeline finds candidates with Tavily (which matches any article
  containing your query terms), then the CURATOR LLM is the gate that's supposed
  to keep only genuine coverage about the company. Its old instruction was just
  "keep genuine third-party content about the company; drop off-topic results"
  — too vague to catch a piece where the company is present but not the subject:
  a passing mention, one name in a round-up, or (your example) a report the
  company authored about a different topic.

  The fix belongs in the curator, not the search — you can't reliably express
  "about vs. merely involves" in a search query, but an LLM can judge it from
  the title + snippet.

  What I changed

  I rewrote the curator prompt around a "primary subject" test, with explicit
  KEEP/DROP rules:

  - KEEP — news/analysis/reviews/commentary whose main subject is the company.
  - DROP even when the company's name appears:
    - passing mentions, or the company as one of many named (listicles, market
  round-ups, "best X of 2026");
    - pieces the company merely authored, published, sponsored, funded, or is 
  quoted in, whose actual subject is a different topic — "a company-authored 
  report about an industry trend is ABOUT that trend, NOT about the company"
  (your exact case);
    - own channels, aggregators, review/ecommerce, off-topic.

  Added a test asserting these criteria are in the prompt; full suite green (160
  backend + 39 frontend).

  To see it

  Backend change → restart ./start_all.sh so the worker reloads, then re-run.
  The curator will now reject the "involves but not about" items.

  Honest caveats

  - This is a prompt/judgment improvement — it makes the curator much stricter
  on aboutness, but it's still an LLM call working from title + snippet, so it
  won't be perfect on borderline cases (e.g. a story genuinely half-about the
  company). Expect noticeably fewer "involves-only" results, not zero.
  - It will reduce item counts somewhat (that's the point — trading volume for
  relevance), which also means slightly fewer points on the sentiment graph.

  If you want a stronger, more auditable version, the next step would be to have
  the curator return an explicit aboutness rating (high/medium/low) per
  accepted item and keep only high/medium — that makes the filter tunable and
  visible rather than baked into one accept/reject decision. Say the word and
  I'll add that; I kept this change minimal and within the existing curator loop
  for now.

      37 +def _flatten_report_text(value):                               
      38 +    """Coerce a report overview (str | dict | list) into readab
         +le prose.                                                      
      39 +                                                               
      40 +    The overview is display text; when a model returns a struct
         +ured object                                                    
      41 +    (e.g. separate positive/negative sections) we flatten it in
         +to labelled                                                    
      42 +    paragraphs rather than failing the whole report (mirrors th
         +e tolerant                                                     
      43 +    IDENTITY handling, §6.1). None -> "" so an empty overview s
         +till fails loud.                                               
      44 +    """                                                        
      45 +    if value is None:                                          
      46 +        return ""                                              
      47 +    if isinstance(value, str):                                 
      48 +        return value                                           
      49 +    if isinstance(value, dict):                                
      50 +        parts = []                                             
      51 +        for key, val in value.items():                         
      52 +            text = _flatten_report_text(val).strip()           
      53 +            if text:                                           
      54 +                label = str(key).replace("_", " ").strip().capi
         +talize()

      59 +            t for t in (_flatten_report_text(v) for v in value)

  My previous change — asking the REPORT to break coverage into
  positive/negative — made the model return executive_overview as a nested JSON 
  object (e.g. {overview, positive: {sources, themes}, negative: {...}}) instead
  of a plain string. The strict parse_report raised MalformedLLMOutput: 
  expected string, got dict, which the fan-in's broad-except caught and degraded
  to the generic "Report generation failed; results below may be partial"
  message. So the run finished, but you lost the actual overview.

  The fix

  Two parts, matching the tolerant approach we used for IDENTITY (the overview
  is display text, not control-flow-critical):

  1. parse_report now flattens a structured overview into prose — a dict becomes
  labelled paragraphs (Positive: …, Negative: …), a list becomes joined
  paragraphs, a plain string passes through unchanged. Only a genuinely
  absent/empty overview still fails loud. Verified against the exact nested
  shape that crashed — it produces clean, readable text.
  2. Prompt nudge — the REPORT prompt now explicitly asks for executive_overview
  as a single plain-text string (not a nested object), using
  Positive:/Negative: lead-ins, so the model nests less often in the first
  place.

  Added tests for the dict and list coercion cases (and kept the "missing/empty
  → fail loud" behavior).

  To see it

  Backend change → restart ./start_all.sh and re-run. The overview will now
  render with its positive/negative sections instead of the failure message.

  One note: this is a good reminder that enriching an LLM prompt can shift its
  output shape, not just its content — I should have anticipated the
  structured-object response when I added the positive/negative breakdown. The
  tolerant parser now guards against that class of regression for the overview
  going forward.

✻ Cooked for 2m 15s

❯ Also, I notice that the summary of a new article does not reflect the         
  identified sentiment. For example, the headline may be quite positive         
  sounding, but the article is negatively rated for sentiment. It is necessary  
  that the summary reflects the indicated sentiment. So, for example, a Reddit  
  post about Apple getting billions in refunds for tariffs... the headline      
  sounds positive, but the sentiment of the commenters was very negative, but   
  you only saw that once clicking into the Reddit page. The summary didn't      
  reflect the sentiment.                                                        

● This is a real quality gap — the displayed summary is Tavily's raw excerpt
  (often just the headline), which can contradict the sentiment (e.g. a
  positive-sounding Reddit title with negative comments). The fix is to have the
  sentiment step produce a sentiment-aware summary from the fuller page content
  and display that. Let me first check whether Tavily already gives us the
  fuller content (I saw a raw_content field earlier).

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Done and committed. Here's what changed, end to end.

  The two problems, and the fix

  1. The scorer only saw the headline/short excerpt. Your Reddit example is the
  giveaway — the negative sentiment lived in the comments, which weren't in the
  300-char snippet. So I enabled Tavily's include_raw_content and now feed the
  sentiment step the full page text (capped at SENTIMENT_MAX_CONTENT_CHARS,
  default 1500 — verified it's flowing: 1500 chars vs the old 300). So the score
  reflects the actual body/comments, not a positive-sounding title.

  2. The displayed summary was Tavily's raw excerpt, not sentiment-aware. The
  SENTIMENT call now also returns a one-sentence summary that reflects the 
  actual sentiment — explicitly told to note things like "commenters are
  negative despite a positive-sounding headline." That summary is stored
  (ContentItem.sentiment_summary, migration 0003) and displayed in place of the 
  raw excerpt — both in the item row and in the scatter-mode tooltip (falling
  back to the raw snippet when a summary is absent).

  Cross-stack: tavily.py → schemas.parse_sentiment (tolerant summary field) →
  pipeline.score_sentiments → model + migration → subtask persistence →
  serializer → ContentItemRow + SentimentGraph. Tests updated throughout; full
  suite green (166 backend + 40 frontend), build clean.

  To see it

  This touches the backend and adds a migration, so:
  1. Restart ./start_all.sh (the migration runs on start; the worker reloads the
  new code).
  2. Start a fresh run (or Refresh) — existing items won't have summaries.

  Honest caveats

  - Cost went up. Feeding ~1500 chars/item (vs 300) roughly 5×'s the sentiment
  call's input tokens, and include_raw_content makes Tavily responses larger.
  It's all bounded by SENTIMENT_MAX_ITEMS (10/category) and the content cap,
  both tunable — lower SENTIMENT_MAX_CONTENT_CHARS if you want to trade some
  accuracy back for cost.
  - It's still an LLM judgment. The summary and score should now line up (both
  come from the same fuller content in one call), but on genuinely ambiguous
  pages it won't be perfect. If you spot a case where the summary still doesn't
  match the score, send me the URL and I'll look — the one place they could
  diverge is if Tavily returns no raw_content for a page (some sites block
  scraping), in which case it falls back to the shorter content.
  - As always, I can't machine-verify the rendered tooltip/summary text in a
  headless test — give it a look on a fresh run, especially a Reddit item, and
  confirm the summary now tells you the real sentiment.  ⎿  Stop says: ✦ 30 memories woven into the palace — sentiment, plan, scored

● The exact failing case now salvages both accepted URLs and stops cleanly. Full

● Fixed and committed.

  What happened

  OpenRouter returned curator JSON that was missing the done field. The strict
  parse_curator raised MalformedLLMOutput: curator missing key: done, which
  propagated to the subtask boundary and marked social_posts red — throwing away
  the entire category's curation (all the accepted URLs) over one missing
  control field.

  The fix

  parse_curator is now tolerant (same approach we applied to IDENTITY and
  REPORT, which hit the identical LLM-variance problem):
  - Salvages accepted — keeps the valid {"url": ...} entries even if the list is
  loosely shaped (drops malformed entries instead of failing).
  - Infers done when it's missing or non-boolean: stop the loop unless a real
  follow-up tool_call.query was requested.
  - Only a genuinely non-JSON body still fails (that's a real error).

  Verified against the exact shape from your log — it now returns both accepted
  URLs with done: True instead of crashing. Full suite green (168 backend + 40
  frontend); updated the curator tests to encode the salvage behavior and added
  a regression test for the missing-done case.

  To see it

  Backend change → restart ./start_all.sh, then re-run. The social_posts (and
  any other) category will keep its results instead of erroring on a missing
  key.

  A broader observation worth flagging

  This is now the fourth LLM call-point I've had to make tolerant against real
  OpenRouter output — IDENTITY (numeric confidence / array matched), REPORT
  (nested object), and CURATOR twice (rejected/duplicates, now done). The plan's
  original §6.1 "strict fail-loud on any schema deviation" has proven too
  brittle for real-world model variance: a single missing/reshaped field
  shouldn't nuke a whole category or run.

  The principled line I've been holding: fail loud only on genuinely non-JSON 
  output (a real fault), but salvage when the JSON is valid and the load-bearing
  data is recoverable. That's now consistent across IDENTITY, REPORT, and
  CURATOR. The two still-strict parsers are QUERY_PLANNER and CATEGORY_SUMMARY —
  if you start seeing failures from those too, tell me and I'll give them the
  same treatment. If you'd like, I can also do a small consolidation pass to
  make this "salvage-valid-JSON" behavior a shared, documented convention rather
  than four separate implementations — say the word.

✻ Churned for 2m 8s

❯ The idea was to use Instructor to automatically check and retry for properly  
  formatted JSON. Only if that fails after some retries should the item itself  
  fail. And I agree: A single failed item shouldn't invalidate the whole        
  category.                                                                     

● I agree with the goal completely: validate the JSON, retry a few times to fix
  transient malformations, and only fail that one item/call (never the whole
  category) if retries are exhausted. That's the right model, and my scattered
  "tolerant parser" patches have been a stopgap toward it.

  Before I build it, one honest engineering call I want to put to you, because
  it materially changes the size and risk of the work. There are two ways to
  deliver exactly the behavior you described:

  Path A — Instructor (the library you named). Add instructor, define Pydantic
  response models for all six call-points, and let the library inject the
  schema, validate, and retry-with-error-feedback. This is the "principled"
  version — but it's an invasive change to the core call_llm seam that every LLM
  call and nearly every test depends on. It means: rewiring the mock seam and
  rewriting most of the LLM-related tests (they currently mock call_llm
  returning a JSON string), re-integrating the §7 logging so each retry is still
  logged, and a new dependency. It's a proper mini-project (I'd want a spec →
  plan → review cycle, like we did for sentiment).

  Path B — a retry wrapper around what we already have. Add a bounded retry loop
  at the call/parse boundary: on a malformed response, re-call the model with 
  the validation error appended ("your last reply was invalid because X; return
  valid JSON matching this shape"), up to LLM_MAX_RETRIES times; only then fall
  back non-fatally per call-point. This gives the same runtime behavior —
  schema-checked, auto-retried, per-item/call failures isolated — using the
  existing seam and parsers (which I'd revert to strict, since retries now
  handle the variance). Far less churn, no new dependency, and I can also make
  each call-point degrade gracefully (query-planner fallback query, curator
  keeps its pool, etc.).

  Both isolate a failed item from the category. The difference is cost/risk, not
  the outcome you asked for. My honest recommendation is B — it's ~20% of the
  work for ~100% of the behavior — but you specifically asked for Instructor,
  and if you want the library's Pydantic-model rigor, I'll do A properly.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Which way should I implement the 
     validate-and-retry-then-fail-the-item-only behavior? → Retry wrapper 
     (recommended)

● The retry wrapper it is. The design:

  - A small call_and_parse(call_fn, name, messages, parse_fn, ...) helper: call
  the model, parse; on MalformedLLMOutput, re-prompt with the validation error
  and retry up to LLM_MAX_RETRIES; raise only if all attempts fail.
  - Wire it into all six structured call-points.
  - Make each call-point non-fatal after retries: sentiment → null (per-item);
  query-planner → fallback query; curator → keep the candidate pool;
  category-summary → None; REPORT → fallback overview. So a category/run never
  dies from LLM output.

  It keeps the existing call_llm mock seam (the helper takes the caller's
  call_llm, which tests already patch), so minimal test churn. Let me build it.

  Step 1 — the retry helper in llm.py.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Done and committed (174 backend + 40 frontend green). Here's what shipped and
  one honest design nuance.

  What I built

  1. A retry wrapper — call_and_parse(call_llm, name, messages, parse_fn, …) —
  calls the model, validates with the parser, and on a MalformedLLMOutput
  re-prompts with the specific validation error ("your last reply was invalid
  because X; return only valid JSON") up to LLM_MAX_RETRIES (default 2), then
  raises. Every attempt is logged, so retries are visible. It takes the caller's
  call_llm so the existing mock seam still works (minimal test churn).
  2. Wired into all six call-points, each with a non-fatal fallback after
  retries are exhausted — so a bad LLM reply never kills a category or run:
    - QUERY_PLANNER → falls back to a basic "<company> <category>" query.
    - CURATOR → keeps the exclusion-filtered candidate pool (don't lose the
  findings).
    - CATEGORY_SUMMARY → summary None.
    - REPORT → a fixed fallback overview, and the run now finalizes normally
  (green/yellow) instead of being degraded just because the prose failed.
    - IDENTITY → already non-fatal (caught by the caller).
    - SENTIMENT → that item gets null sentiment (per-item, exactly your "only
  the item fails" rule).

  New env LLM_MAX_RETRIES (documented in .env-example); tests cover
  retry-then-succeed, retry-then-raise, and each fallback.

  The design nuance (worth a look)

  I did not make every parser strict-and-retry. Where the parser can coerce 
  cheaply and losslessly, it still does so without spending a retry — clamping a
  sentiment score to [−1, 1], deriving the label from the score, inferring done
  when omitted, flattening a nested overview to prose. Only genuine 
  structural/non-JSON errors trigger the retry loop and then the fallback.

  I think this hybrid is actually better than pure strict-retry — it avoids
  extra LLM calls (and cost) for variance we can safely interpret, and reserves
  retries for real errors. But it's a deliberate deviation from a literal
  "always validate strictly, then retry, then fail." If you'd rather have the
  pure model — strict schemas everywhere, so any deviation forces a retry — say
  so and I'll flip the tolerant parsers back to strict (the wrapper + fallbacks
  are already in place, so it's a small change).

  To see it

  Backend change → restart ./start_all.sh, then run. Transient malformed replies
  (like the missing done you hit) now self-correct via retry or salvage, and
  nothing fails a whole category.

  On process

  This touched the core LLM-calling path across pipeline/identity/tasks. I
  self-verified the key risks (mock seam intact, fallbacks non-fatal, JSON mode
  preserved, network errors still degrade as before) and the full suite passes —
  but unlike the sentiment feature I didn't run the multi-round clear-context
  adversarial review this time, since it came in as a direct request rather than
  a /implement. If you'd like, I'll run a couple of adversarial review rounds
  over this change specifically before you rely on it. Your call.
  ⎿  Stop says: ✦ 30 memories woven into the palace — sentiment, plan, scored
✻ Churned for 9m 45s

※ recap: We're building the Drumbeat company-research app and just added an LLM retry wrapper with non-fatal fallbacks so bad model replies never fail a category. Next: optionally run an adversarial review of that change, or restart start_all.sh and test it.
